# BOUNDS CONSISTENT

## 6.2.6 Sudoku example

The popular `Sudoku` puzzle has introduced millions of people to constraint satisfaction problems, although they may not recognize it. A Sudoku board consists of 81 squares, some of which are initially filled with digits from 1 to 9. The puzzle is to fill in all the remaining squares such that no digit appears twice in any row, column, or 3 × 3 box (see Figure 6.4). A row, column, or box is called a `unit`.

The Sudoku puzzles that are printed in newspapers and puzzle books have the property that there is exactly one solution. Although some can be tricky to solve by hand, taking tens of minutes, even the hardest Sudoku problems yield to a CSP solver in less than 0.1 second.

A Sudoku puzzle can be considered a CSP with 81 variables, one for each square. We use the variable names `A1` through `A9` for the top row (left to right), down to `I1` through `I9` for the bottom row. The empty squares have the domain {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9} and the prefilled squares have a domain consisting of a single value. In addition, there are 27 different

> ![image](img/00155.jpg)

> **Figure 6.4** (a) A Sudoku puzzle and (b) its solution.

`Alldiff` constraints: one for each row, column, and box of 9 squares.

> ![equ](img/00156.jpg)

Let us see how far arc consistency can take us. Assume that the `Alldiff` constraints have been expanded into binary constraints (such as `A1` ≠ `A2` ) so that we can apply the AC-3 algorithm directly. Consider variable `E6` from Figure 6.4(a)—the empty square between the 2 and the 8 in the middle box. From the constraints in the box, we can remove not only 2 and 8 but also 1 and 7 from `E6`’s domain. From the constraints in its column, we can eliminate 5, 6, 2, 8, 9, and 3. That leaves `E6` with a domain of {4}; in other words, we know the answer for `E6`. Now consider variable `I6` —the square in the bottom middle box surrounded by 1, 3, and 3. Applying arc consistency in its column, we eliminate 5, 6, 2, 4 (since we now know `E6` must be 4), 8, 9, and 3. We eliminate 1 by arc consistency with `I5`, and we are left with only the value 7 in the domain of `I6`. Now there are 8 known values in column 6, so arc consistency can infer that `A6` must be 1. Inference continues along these lines, and eventually, AC-3 can solve the entire puzzle—all the variables have their domains reduced to a single value, as shown in Figure 6.4(b).

Of course, Sudoku would soon lose its appeal if every puzzle could be solved by a mechanical application of AC-3, and indeed AC-3 works only for the easiest Sudoku puzzles. Slightly harder ones can be solved by PC-2, but at a greater computational cost: there are 255,960 different path constraints to consider in a Sudoku puzzle. To solve the hardest puzzles and to make efficient progress, we will have to be more clever.

Indeed, the appeal of Sudoku puzzles for the human solver is the need to be resourceful in applying more complex inference strategies. Aficionados give them colorful names, such as “naked triples.” That strategy works as follows: in any unit (row, column or box), find three squares that each have a domain that contains the same three numbers or a subset of those numbers. For example, the three domains might be {1, 8}, {3, 8}, and {1, 3, 8}. From that we don’t know which square contains 1, 3, or 8, but we do know that the three numbers must be distributed among the three squares. Therefore we can remove 1, 3, and 8 from the domains of every *other* square in the unit.

It is interesting to note how far we can go without saying much that is specific to Sudoku. We do of course have to say that there are 81 variables, that their domains are the digits 1 to 9, and that there are 27 `Alldiff` constraints. But beyond that, all the strategies—arc consistency, path consistency, etc.—apply generally to all CSPs, not just to Sudoku problems. Even naked triples is really a strategy for enforcing consistency of `Alldiff` constraints and has nothing to do with Sudoku *per se*. This is the power of the CSP formalism: for each new problem area, we only need to define the problem in terms of constraints; then the general constraint-solving mechanisms can take over.

### 6.3 BACKTRACKING SEARCH FOR CSPS

Sudoku problems are designed to be solved by inference over constraints. But many other CSPs cannot be solved by inference alone; there comes a time when we must search for a solution. In this section we look at backtracking search algorithms that work on partial assignments; in the next section we look at local search algorithms over complete assignments.

We could apply a standard depth-limited search (from Chapter 3). A state would be a partial assignment, and an action would be adding `var = value` to the assignment. But for a CSP with `n` variables of domain size `d`, we quickly notice something terrible: the branching factor at the top level is `n*d` because any of `d` values can be assigned to any of `n` variables. At the next level, the branching factor is `(n - 1)*d`, and so on for `n` levels. We generate a tree with `n! * d^n` leaves, even though there are only `d^n` possible complete assignments!

Our seemingly reasonable but naive formulation ignores crucial property common to all CSPs: **commutativity**. A problem is commutative if the order of application of any given set of actions has no effect on the outcome. CSPs are commutative because when assigning values to variables, we reach the same partial assignment regardless of order. Therefore, we need only consider a `single` variable at each node in the search tree. For example, at the root node of a search tree for coloring the map of Australia, we might make a choice between `SA = red`, `SA = green`, and `SA = blue`, but we would never choose between `SA = red` and `WA = blue`. With this restriction, the number of leaves is `d^n`, as we would hope.

COMMUTATIVITY

> ![image](img/00157.jpg)

> **Figure 6.5** A simple backtracking algorithm for constraint satisfaction problems. The algorithm is modeled on the recursive depth-first search of Chapter 3. By varying the functions SELECT-UNASSIGNED-VARIABLE and ORDER-DOMAIN-VALUES, we can implement the general-purpose heuristics discussed in the text. The function INFERENCE can optionally be used to impose arc-, path-, or `k`-consistency, as desired. If a value choice leads to failure (noticed either by INFERENCE or by BACKTRACK), then value assignments (including those made by INFERENCE) are removed from the current assignment and a new value is tried



The term `backtracking search` is used for a depth-first search that chooses values for one variable at a time and backtracks when a variable has no legal values left to assign. The algorithm is shown in Figure 6.5. It repeatedly chooses an unassigned variable, and then tries all values in the domain of that variable in turn, trying to find a solution. If an inconsistency is detected, then `BACKTRACK` returns failure, causing the previous call to try another value. Part of the search tree for the Australia problem is shown in Figure 6.6, where we have assigned variables in the order `WA`, `NT`, `Q`, …. Because the representation of CSPs is standardized, there is no need to supply `BACKTRACKING-SEARCH` with a domain-specific initial state, action function, transition model, or goal test.

* * *

`BACKTRACKING SEARCH`

* * *

Notice that `BACKTRACKING-SEARCH` keeps only a single representation of a state and alters that representation rather than creating new ones, as described on page 87.

In Chapter 3 we improved the poor performance of uninformed search algorithms by supplying them with domain-specific heuristic functions derived from our knowledge of the problem. It turns out that we can solve CSPs efficiently *without* such domain-specific knowledge. Instead, we can add some sophistication to the unspecified functions in Figure 6.5, using them to address the following questions:

> 1\. Which variable should be assigned next (`SELECT-UNASSIGNED-VARIABLE`), and in what order should its values be tried (`ORDER-DOMAIN-VALUES`)?

> ![image](img/00158.jpg)

> **Figure 6.6** Part of the search tree for the map-coloring problem in Figure 6.1.

> 2\. What inferences should be performed at each step in the search (`INFERENCE`)?

> 3\. When the search arrives at an assignment that violates a constraint, can the search avoid repeating this failure?

The subsections that follow answer each of these questions in turn.

#### **6.3.1 Variable and value ordering**

The backtracking algorithm contains the line

> *var* ← `SELECT-UNASSIGNED-VARIABLE`(`csp`).

The simplest strategy for `SELECT-UNASSIGNED-VARIABLE` is to choose the next unassigned variable in order, {*X*[1], *X*[2], …}. This static variable ordering seldom results in the most efficient search. For example, after the assignments for `WA` = `red` and `NT` = `green` in Figure 6.6, there is only one possible value for `SA`, so it makes sense to assign `SA` = `blue` next rather than assigning `Q`. In fact, after `SA` is assigned, the choices for `Q`, `NSW`, and `V` are all forced. This intuitive idea—choosing the variable with the fewest “legal” values—is called the **minimum-remaining-values** (MRV) heuristic. It also has been called the “most constrained variable” or “fail-first” heuristic, the latter because it picks a variable that is most likely to cause a failure soon, thereby pruning the search tree. If some variable *X* has no legal values left, the MRV heuristic will select *X* and failure will be detected immediately—avoiding pointless searches through other variables. The MRV heuristic usually performs better than a random or static ordering, sometimes by a factor of 1,000 or more, although the results vary widely depending on the problem.

* * *

`MINIMUM-REMAINING-VALUES`

* * *

The MRV heuristic doesn’t help at all in choosing the first region to color in Australia, because initially every region has three legal colors. In this case, the **degree heuristic** comes in handy. It attempts to reduce the branching factor on future choices by selecting the variable that is involved in the largest number of constraints on other unassigned variables. In Figure 6.1, `SA` is the variable with highest degree, 5; the other variables have degree 2 or 3, except for `T`, which has degree 0. In fact, once `SA` is chosen, applying the degree heuristic solves the problem without any false steps—you can choose *any* consistent color at each choice point and still arrive at a solution with no backtracking. The minimum-remaining-values heuristic is usually a more powerful guide, but the degree heuristic can be useful as a tiebreaker.

* * *

`DEGREE HEURISTIC`

* * *

Once a variable has been selected, the algorithm must decide on the order in which to examine its values. For this, the **least-constraining-value** heuristic can be effective in some cases. It prefers the value that rules out the fewest choices for the neighboring variables in the constraint graph. For example, suppose that in Figure 6.1 we have generated the partial assignment with `WA` = `red` and `NT` = `green` and that our next choice is for `Q`. Blue would be a bad choice because it eliminates the last legal value left for `Q`’s neighbor, `SA`. The least-constraining-value heuristic therefore prefers red to blue. In general, the heuristic is trying to leave the maximum flexibility for subsequent variable assignments. Of course, if we are trying to find all the solutions to a problem, not just the first one, then the ordering does not matter because we have to consider every value anyway. The same holds if there are no solutions to the problem.

* * *

`LEAST-CONSTRAINING-VALUE`

* * *

Why should variable selection be fail-first, but value selection be fail-last? It turns out that, for a wide variety of problems, a variable ordering that chooses a variable with the minimum number of remaining values helps minimize the number of nodes in the search tree by pruning larger parts of the tree earlier. For value ordering, the trick is that we only need one solution; therefore it makes sense to look for the most likely values first. If we wanted to enumerate all solutions rather than just find one, then value ordering would be irrelevant.

#### **6.3.2 Interleaving search and inference**

So far we have seen how AC-3 and other algorithms can infer reductions in the domain of variables *before* we begin the search. But inference can be even more powerful in the course of a search: every time we make a choice of a value for a variable, we have a brand-new opportunity to infer new domain reductions on the neighboring variables.

One of the simplest forms of inference is called **forward checking**. Whenever a variable *X* is assigned, the forward-checking process establishes arc consistency for it: for each unassigned variable *Y* that is connected to *X* by a constraint, delete from *Y*’s domain any value that is inconsistent with the value chosen for *X*. Because forward checking only does arc consistency inferences, there is no reason to do forward checking if we have already done arc consistency as a preprocessing step.

* * *

`FORWARD CHECKING`

* * *

Figure 6.7 shows the progress of backtracking search on the Australia CSP with forward checking. There are two important points to notice about this example. First, notice that after `WA` = `red` and `Q` = `green` are assigned, the domains of `NT` and `SA` are reduced to a single value; we have eliminated branching on these variables altogether by propagating information from `WA` and `Q`. A second point to notice is that after `V` = `blue`, the domain of `SA` is empty. Hence, forward checking has detected that the partial assignment {`WA` = `red`, `Q` = `green`, `V` = `blue`} is inconsistent with the constraints of the problem, and the algorithm will therefore backtrack immediately.



-   结合 MRV 启发式与前向检查的搜索对于许多问题更有效。考虑在分配`{*WA* = *red*}`之后的[Figure 6.7。直观上，该分配似乎会限制其邻居`*NT*`和`*SA*`，因此我们应该先处理这些变量，然后其余变量就会迎刃而解。这正是 MRV 的工作方式：`*NT*`和`*SA*`各有两个值，因此先选其中一个，再选另一个，然后依次处理`*Q*`、`*NSW*`和`*V*`。最后`*T*`仍有三个值，任意一个都可以。我们可以将前向检查视为一种高效的方式，用于逐步计算 MRV 启发式所需的信息。

> ![image](img/00159.jpg)

> **Figure 6.7** 使用前向检查的地图涂色搜索进度。先分配`*WA* = *red*`；随后前向检查从邻居变量`*NT*`和`*SA*`的域中删除红色。在分配`*Q* = *green*`后，绿色从`*NT*`、`*SA*`和`*NSW*`的域中被删除。在分配`*V* = *blue*`后，蓝色从`*NSW*`和`*SA*`的域中被删除，使得`*SA*`没有合法值。

尽管前向检查能够检测出许多不一致性，但它并不能检测出所有不一致。问题在于它只使当前变量具有弧一致性，而不去检查其他变量的弧一致性。例如，考虑 Figure 6.7 的第三行，显示当`*WA*`为*red*且`*Q*`为*green*时，`*NT*`和`*SA*`都被迫为蓝色。前向检查不足以发现这是一种不一致：`*NT*`和`*SA*`相邻，因此不能取相同的值。

名为 MAC（维持弧一致性 MAC）的算法能够检测到这种不一致。变量`*X[i]*`被赋值后，INFERENCE 过程会调用 AC-3，但不再使用包含所有 CSP 弧的队列，而是仅从`*X[i]*`的邻居中未赋值变量的弧`(*X[j]*, *X[i]*)`开始。随后 AC-3 按常规方式进行约束传播，若任何变量的域被缩减至空集，则 AC-3 调用失败，立即回溯。可以看出 MAC 比前向检查更强大，因为前向检查仅在 MAC 的队列中对初始弧执行与 MAC 相同的操作；而 MAC 能够在变量域变化时递归传播约束。

* * *

MAINTAINING ARC CONSISTENCY (MAC)

* * *

#### **6.3.3 智能回溯：向后回溯**

Figure 6.5 中的 BACKTRACKING-SEARCH 算法在搜索分支失败时采用最简单的策略：向后退到前一个变量并尝试其它值。这被称为**按顺序回溯**，因为它会重新访问**最新**的决策点。本节考虑更好的选择。

* * *

CHRONOLOGICAL BACKTRACKING

* * *

考虑在 Figure 6.1 中，使用固定变量顺序`Q`, `NSW`, `V`, `T`, `SA`, `WA`, `NT`并进行简单回溯的情形。假设已生成部分赋值`{*Q* = *red*, *NSW* = *green*, *V* = *blue*, *T* = *red*}`。当尝试下一个变量`SA`时，发现所有值都违背约束。于是回退到`T`并尝试新的颜色！显然这很愚蠢——重新给塔斯马尼亚上色不可能解决南澳大利亚的问题。

更智能的回溯方式是回到可能解决问题的变量——即导致`SA`某些值不可行的变量。为此，我们维护一个**冲突集**，包含与`SA`某个值冲突的赋值集合（本例为`{*Q* = *red*, *NSW* = *green*, *V* = *blue*}`）。**回跳**（backjumping）方法回溯到冲突集中最新的赋值；在此例中，回跳会跳过塔斯马尼亚，转而尝试`V`的新值。该方法只需在 BACKTRACK 过程中累积冲突集即可实现。

* * *

CONFLICT SET

* * *

* * *

BACKJUMPING

* * *

细心的读者会注意到，前向检查可以在无额外工作的情况下提供冲突集：每当前向检查基于赋值`*X* = *x*`删除`*Y*`域中的值时，应将`*X* = *x*`加入`*Y*`的冲突集。若`*Y*`域的最后一个值被删除，则`*Y*`的冲突集中的赋值会加入`*X*`的冲突集。这样在处理`*Y*`时，就能立即知道回溯到哪里。

聪明的读者还会发现一个奇怪之处：回跳发生在变量域的所有值都与当前赋值冲突时，而前向检查已经在出现这种情况之前就阻止了搜索到达该节点！事实上，可以证明**所有**由回跳剪枝的分支也会被前向检查剪枝。因此，在前向检查或更强的弧一致性（如 MAC）搜索中，简单回跳是多余的。

尽管有上述观察，回跳的思想仍然有价值：根据失败的原因进行回溯。回跳在变量域为空时检测到失败，但在许多情况下，分支在域为空之前就已经注定失败。再次考虑部分赋值`{*WA* = *red*, *NSW* = *red*}`（前文已说明这是不一致的）。若随后尝试`*T* = *red*`，再依次为`*NT*`, `*Q*`, `*V*`, `*SA*`赋值，我们知道这四个变量的任何组合都无法满足约束，最终会在`*NT*`耗尽所有可尝试的值。此时该回溯到哪里？回跳无法工作，因为`*NT*`仍然有与已赋值变量一致的值——它没有完整的冲突集导致失败。我们需要考虑的是`*NT*`、`*Q*`、`*V*`和`*SA*`这四个变量**整体**导致的失败，这由它们直接冲突的前置变量集合决定。在此例中，冲突集合是`*WA*`和`*NSW*`，因此算法应回溯到`*NSW*`并跳过塔斯马尼亚。使用冲突集合的回跳算法称作**冲突导向回跳**（conflict-directed backjumping）。

* * *

CONFLICT-DIRECTED BACKJUMPING

* * *




我们现在需要解释这些新的冲突集是如何计算的。该方法实际上非常简单。分支搜索的“终端”失败总是因为变量的取值域为空；该变量有一个标准冲突集。在我们的例子中，SA 失败，其冲突集是（例如）{*WA*, *NT*, *Q*）。我们回退到 *Q*，并 *Q* 将除 *Q* 本身之外的 *SA* 的冲突集（即 {*NT*, *NSW*）吸收进它自己的直接冲突集，新的冲突集为 {*WA*, *NT*, *NSW*}，也就是说从 *Q* 往后不存在解，前提是之前对 {*WA*, *NT*, *NSW*} 进行了赋值。因此，我们回退到 *NT*，即这些变量中最近的一个。*NT* 将 {*WA*, *NT*, *NSW*} − {*NT*} 吸收进它自己的直接冲突集 {*WA*}，得到 {*WA*, *NSW*）（如上文所述）。随后算法回退到 *NSW*，正如我们所希望的。总结一下：令 *X[j]* 为当前变量，令 conf (*X[j]*) 为其冲突集。如果 *X[j]* 的所有可能取值都失败，则回退到冲突集 *conf* (*X[j]*) 中最近的变量 *X[i]*，并设置

> ![equ](img/00160.jpg)

当我们遇到矛盾时，回退可以告诉我们需要后退多远，从而避免浪费时间修改不会解决问题的变量。我们也希望避免再次出现相同的问题。当搜索到达矛盾时，我们知道冲突集的某个子集导致了问题。**约束学习**是找出冲突集中最小的变量子集的想法，这些变量及其对应的取值构成一个 **无良策**（no-good）。我们记录该无良策，要么通过向 CSP 添加新约束，要么保留单独的缓存。

* * *

CONSTRAINT LEARNING

* * *

* * *

NO-GOOD

* * *

例如，考虑在 Figure 6.6 底部行的状态 {*WA* = *red*, *NT* = *green*, *Q* = *blue*}。前向检查可以告诉我们此状态是无良策，因为 *SA* 没有合法赋值。在这种情况下，记录无良策并没有帮助，因为一旦我们从搜索树中剪除这条分支，我们将永远不会再次遇到这种组合。但假设 Figure 6.6 中的搜索树实际上是从先为 *V* 和 *T* 赋值的更大搜索树的一部分，那么记录 {*WA* = *red*, *NT* = *green*, *Q* = *blue*} 作为无良策是有价值的，因为我们在 *V* 和 *T* 的每种可能赋值下都会再次遇到相同的问题。

无良策可以通过前向检查或回退跳跃有效使用。约束学习是现代 CSP 求解器用于在复杂问题上实现高效的最重要技术之一。

### 6.4 LOCAL SEARCH FOR CSPS

局部搜索算法（见 Section 4.1）在解决许多 CSP 时表现有效。它们使用完整状态形式 ulation：初始状态为每个变量赋值，搜索每次更改一个变量的值。例如，在 8-皇后问题（见 Figure 4.3）中，初始状态可能是 8 个皇后在 8 列中的随机配置，每一步移动一个皇后到其列中的新位置。初始配置通常违反了多个约束。局部搜索的目标是消除这些被违反的约束。

在为变量选择新值时，最直观的启发式是选择导致与其他变量冲突最少的值——**minconflicts** 启发式。算法如图 6.8 所示，其在 8-皇后问题中的应用如图 6.9 所示。

> ![image](img/00161.jpg)

> **Figure 6.8** 用局部搜索求解 CSP 的 MIN-CONFLICTS 算法。初始状态可以随机选择，也可以通过贪心过程选择每个变量的最小冲突值。CONFLICTS 函数统计给定当前赋值下某个值违反的约束数量。

* * *

MINCONFLICTS

* * *

> ![image](img/00162.jpg)

> **Figure 6.9** 使用 minconflicts 对 8-皇后问题的两步解法示意。每一步都会在其列中选择一个皇后进行重新分配。每个方格中显示的冲突数（即攻击的皇后数量）。算法将皇后移动到冲突最少的方格，并在平局时随机处理。

minconflicts 对许多 CSP  surprisingly 有效。令人惊讶的是，在 n-皇后问题上（不计皇后的初始放置），minconflicts 的运行时间大约与问题规模无关。它平均只需 50 步即可解决甚至百万皇后的问题（初始放置后）。这一观察在 1990 年代激发了大量关于局部搜索及易硬问题的研究，我们将在 Chapter 7 中讨论。直观而言，n-皇后问题对局部搜索容易，因为解在状态空间中分布密集。minconflicts 也能很好地处理硬问题。例如，它已用于哈勃太空望远镜的观测调度，将调度一周的时间从三周（！）缩短到约 10 分钟。

所有来自 Section 4.1 的局部搜索技术均可应用于 CSP，且部分技术已被证明特别有效。在 minconflicts 启发式下，CSP 的状态空间通常呈现一系列高原。可能有数百万个仅与解相差一个冲突的变量赋值。高原搜索——允许在相同评分的状态之间进行横向移动——可以帮助局部搜索脱离高原。这种在高原上的漫游可以通过 **tabu search**（保持一小列最近访问的状态并禁止返回这些状态）来引导。

另一种技术叫 **constraint weighting**，可以帮助集中搜索在重要约束上。每个约束最初赋予一个数值权重 *W[i]*，全部为 1。在搜索的每一步，算法选择一个变量/值对，使其导致所有被违反约束的总权重最小。随后，违反约束的权重会递增。这带来两个好处：它为高原增加地形，确保从当前状态可以改进；同时，随着时间的推移，它会给那些难以解决的约束加权，使其更易处理。

* * *

CONSTRAINT WEIGHTING

* * *

局部搜索的另一个优势是可以在在线环境中使用，当问题发生变化时尤其有用，这在调度问题中尤为重要。例如，一周的航班调度可能涉及数千个航班和数万名人员的安排，但若某机场出现恶劣天气，调度可能变得不可行。我们可以使用局部搜索算法从当前调度开始进行修复，只需最小改动。使用回溯搜索重新设定约束通常耗时更长，并且可能产生比局部搜索更多的改动。

### 6.5 THE STRUCTURE OF PROBLEMS




在这一节中，我们考察如何利用问题的`结构`——即由约束图表示的结构——快速找到解。这里的大多数方法也适用于除了约束满足度问题（CSP）之外的其他问题，例如概率推理。毕竟，我们唯一可能希望处理现实世界的方式是将其分解为许多子问题。再次观察澳大利亚的约束图（Figure 6.1(b)，再次作为 Figure 6.12(a)），一个显著事实是：塔斯马尼亚与大陆不相连。直观上，这很明显：给大陆上色和给塔斯马尼亚上色是独立的子问题——任何大陆的解与任何塔斯马尼亚的解结合，都能构成整个地图的解。独立性可以通过在约束图中找到连通分量来判断。每个分量对应一个子问题`CSP[i]`。如果`S[i]`是`CSP[i]`的解，那么`![image](img/00163.jpg)`是`![image](img/00164.jpg)`的解。这很重要，因为考虑以下情况：假设每个`CSP[i]`包含`c`个变量，而总变量数为`n`，其中`c`是常数。那么就有`n/c`个子问题，每个子问题最多需要`d^c`的工作来解决，其中`d`是域的大小。因此，总工作量为`O(d^cn/c)`，这是线性的`n`；如果不分解，总工作量为`O(d^n)`，这是指数级的`n`。让我们具体化这一点：将包含 80 个变量的布尔 CSP 分成四个子问题，最坏情况的解时间从宇宙寿命缩短到不到一秒。

* * *

INDEPENDENT SUBPROBLEMS

* * *

* * *

CONNECTED COMPONENT

* * *

完全独立的子问题很美味，但很少见。好在还有其他图结构也容易解决。例如，当约束图中任何两个变量之间只有一条路径时，该约束图是一棵树。我们展示任何树结构的 CSP 都可以在与变量数量线性的时间内解决。关键是一个新的概念——一致性，称为有向弧一致性（DAC）。如果在变量顺序`X[1]`、`X[2]`、`…`、`X[n]`下，CSP 被定义为有向弧一致，当且仅当每个`X[i]`都与每个`X[j]`（其中`j > i`）一致。

* * *

DIRECTED ARC CONSISTENCY

* * *

![hand](img/00006.jpg)

要解决树结构的 CSP，首先选择任意变量作为树的根，并选择一个变量顺序，使得每个变量在树中出现在其父节点之后。这种顺序称为拓扑排序。Figure 6.10(a)展示了一个样本树和(b)展示了一个可能的顺序。每棵包含 n 个节点的树有 n−1 条弧，因此我们可以在 O(n)步骤内使该图有向弧一致，每个步骤需比较两个变量的最多 d 个域值，总时间为 O(nd²)。一旦我们得到一个有向弧一致的图，就可以按变量列表从上到下遍历，选择剩余的值。由于父节点到子节点的每条弧都是一致的，我们可以确定对于父节点的任何值，子节点总会有剩余的合法值可选。这意味着我们不需要回溯；可以线性地遍历变量。完整的算法在 Figure 6.11 中给出。

* * *

TOPOLOGICAL SORT

* * *

> ![image](img/00165.jpg)

> **Figure 6.10** (a) The constraint graph of a tree-structured CSP. (b) A linear ordering of the variables consistent with the tree with A as the root. This is known as a topological sort of the variables.

现在我们有了树的高效算法，可以考虑更一般的约束图是否可以通过某种方式简化为树。有两种主要方法，一种是删除节点，另一种是合并节点。

第一种方法是为一些变量赋值，使剩余变量形成树。考虑澳大利亚的约束图（再次在 Figure 6.12(a)中显示）。如果我们能删除萨 outh Australia（SA），图就会变成树，如(b)所示。幸运的是，我们可以这样做（在图中，而不是大陆）通过固定 SA 的值，并从其他变量的域中删除与 SA 值不一致的值。

> ![image](img/00166.jpg)

> **Figure 6.11** 树结构 CSP 求解算法（TREE-CSP-SOLVER）。如果 CSP 有解，我们将在线性时间内找到它；如果没有，我们将检测到矛盾。

> ![image](img/00167.jpg)

> **Figure 6.12** (a) 原始约束图（来自 Figure 6.1）。(b) 删除 SA 后的约束图。

因此，删除 SA 及其约束后的 CSP 的任何解都将与为 SA 选择的值保持一致。（这适用于二元 CSP；高阶约束的情况更为复杂。）因此，我们可以使用上述算法解决剩余的树问题，从而解决整个问题。当然，在一般情况下（与地图涂色不同），为 SA 选择的值可能是错误的，因此我们需要尝试 SA 的每一个可能值。一般算法如下：

> 1. 选择一个变量子集 S，使得在删除 S 后，约束图变成树。S 称为循环切割集（cycle cutset）。

* * *

CYCLE CUTSET

* * *

> 2. 对于 S 中变量的每一个可能赋值（满足 S 上的所有约束），

> (a) 从剩余变量的域中删除与 S 的赋值不一致的值，

> (b) 如果剩余 CSP 有解，则返回该解并连同 S 的赋值一起。

如果循环切割集的大小为 c，那么总运行时间为`O(d^c (n − c)d²)`：我们需要尝试 S 中变量的`d^c`种值的组合，对于每种组合，我们需要解决一个大小为`n − c`的树问题。如果图是“几乎树形”的，那么 c 会很小，相对于直截了当回溯，节省会非常大。然而，在最坏情况下，c 可以达到`(n − 2)`。找到最小的循环切割集是 NP 难的，但已知几种高效的近似算法。这种整体算法方法被称为切割集条件化（cutset conditioning），并在第 14 章中再次出现，用于处理概率推理。

* * *

CUTSET CONDITIONING

* * *

第二种方法基于构建约束图的树分解，将其分解为一组连通的子问题。每个子问题独立求解，然后将结果解合并。像大多数分治算法一样，这在子问题不太大时效果很好。Figure 6.13 展示了地图涂色问题的树分解，分为五个子问题。树分解必须满足以下三个要求：

* * *

TREE DECOMPOSITION

* * *

> • 每个原始问题中的变量至少出现在一个子问题中。

> • 如果两个变量在原始问题中由约束连接，它们必须在至少一个子问题中一起出现（连同约束）。

> • 如果一个变量出现在两个子问题中，它必须出现在连接这两个子问题的树路径上的所有子问题中。




前两个条件确保所有变量和约束都在分解中得到表示。第三个条件看起来相当技术性，但本质上反映了约束：任何给定变量在其出现的每一个子问题中都必须取相同的值；用于连接子问题的链接强制执行了这一约束。例如，`SA` 出现在 Figure 6.13 中四个相连的子问题的全部。你可以从 Figure 6.12 中验证，这种分解是合理的。

我们独立解决每个子问题；如果任何一个子问题没有解，则整个问题无解。如果我们能够解决所有子问题，则按照以下方式尝试构建全局解：首先，我们将每个子问题视为一个“超变量”，其域为该子问题的所有解的集合。例如，Figure 6.13 中最左侧的子问题是一个地图涂色问题，包含三个变量，因此有六种解法——其中一种是 {`WA` = `red`, `SA` = `blue`, `NT` = `green`}。然后，我们使用前文给出的树形高效算法解决子问题之间的约束。子问题之间的约束仅要求子问题的解在共享变量上保持一致。例如，给定子问题 {`WA` = `red`, `SA` = `blue`, `NT` = `green`} 的解，下一个子问题唯一可行的解是 {`SA` = `blue`, `NT` = `green`, `Q` = `red`}。

* * *

TREE WIDTH

* * *

一个约束图可以有许多树分解；在选择分解时，目标是使子问题尽可能小。树分解的**树宽**是最大子问题大小减一；图本身的树宽定义为所有树分解中最小的树宽。如果一个图的树宽为 w，并且给出了相应的树分解，则问题可以在 *O*(*nd*^(*w*+1)) 时间内求解。因此，*树宽受限的约束满足度问题（CSP）是可在多项式时间内求解的。* 遗憾的是，找到最小树宽的分解是 NP-难的，但有许多启发式方法在实践中表现良好。

> ![image](img/00168.jpg)

> **Figure 6.13** 约束图在 Figure 6.12 中的 (a) 的树分解。

![hand](img/00006.jpg)

我们目前已经观察了约束图的结构。变量的取值也可能包含重要的结构。考虑使用 *n* 种颜色的地图涂色问题。对于每一个一致的解，实际上存在由颜色名称进行全排列形成的 *n*! 种解。例如，在澳大利亚地图中，WA、NT 和 SA 必须使用不同的颜色，但这三个区域有 3! = 6 种颜色的排列方式。这称为**值对称性**。我们希望通过破坏对称性，将搜索空间缩小一个因子 *n*!。为此，我们引入**对称破坏约束**。例如，我们可以强加任意的顺序约束，如 *NT* < *SA* < *WA*，要求三个值按字母顺序排列。该约束确保仅有一种 *n*! 解可行：{`NT` = `blue`, `SA` = `green`, `WA` = `red`}。

* * *

VALUE SYMMETRY

* * *

* * *

SYMMETRY-BREAKING CONSTRAINT

* * *

对于地图涂色问题，很容易找到能消除对称性的约束，而且在一般情况下，可以在多项式时间内找到约束，使得仅剩下一个对称解。但在这一搜索过程中的中间值集合上消除所有对称性是 NP-难的。实际上，破坏值对称性在广泛的问题上被证明是重要且有效的。

### 6.6 SUMMARY

> • **约束满足度问题**（CSP）通过一组变量/取值对表示状态，并通过一组约束表示解的条件。许多重要的实际问题可以描述为 CSP。

> • 多种推理技术利用约束推断哪些变量/取值对是一致的、哪些不一致，包括节点一致性、弧一致性、路径一致性和 *k*-一致性。

> • **回溯搜索**，一种深度优先搜索的形式，常用于解决 CSP。推理可以与搜索相互交织。

> • **最小剩余值**和**度**启发式是域无关的方法，用于在回溯搜索中决定下一步选择哪个变量。最小约束值启发式有助于决定对给定变量尝试哪个值。当无法为变量找到合法赋值时会发生回溯。**冲突引导回溯跳跃**直接回到问题的根源进行回溯。

> • 使用**minconflicts**启发式的本地搜索也已成功应用于约束满足度问题。

> • 解决 CSP 的复杂度与其约束图的结构强相关。树形问题可以在线性时间内求解。**切集条件化**可以将一般 CSP 降为树形，如果能找到小切集，则非常高效。**树分解**技术将 CSP 转换为子问题的树，如果约束图的**树宽**较小，则非常高效。

### BIBLIOGRAPHICAL AND HISTORICAL NOTES

约束满足度最早的研究主要针对数值约束。印度数学家 Brahmagupta 在七世纪研究了整数域的等式约束，这些通常被称为**狄奥芬特方程**，以希腊数学家 Diophantus（约 200–284）命名，他实际上考虑了正有理数的域。高斯（1829）研究了通过变量消去求解线性方程的系统方法；线性不等式约束的解法可追溯至傅里叶（1827）。

* * *

DIOPHANTINE EQUATIONS

* * *

有限域约束满足度问题也有悠久的历史。例如，**图染色**（其中地图染色是特例）是数学中的古老问题。四色猜想（即平面图可以用四种或更少颜色染色）最初由 Francis Guthrie（德摩根的学生）于 1852 年提出。该猜想历经多年未解，直到 Appel 和 Haken（1977）提出证明（参见书籍 *Four Colors Suffice* (Wilson, 2004)）。严格的数学家们对证明中部分依赖计算机感到失望，2008 年 Georges Gonthier 使用 COQ 定理证明器推导了正式证明，验证了 Appel 和 Haken 的证明是正确的。

* * *

GRAPH COLORING

* * *

约束满足度的具体类别在计算机科学的历史中屡见不鲜。最具影响力的早期例子之一是 1963 年 Sutherland 的 SKETCHPAD 系统，它解决了图中几何约束，是现代绘图程序和 CAD 工具的前身。CSP 被视为*通用*类的识别是由 Ugo Montanari（1974）完成的。将高阶 CSP 降为仅包含二元约束且使用辅助变量的纯二元 CSP（参见练习 6.6）最初由 19 世纪逻辑学家 Charles Sanders Peirce 提出，后由 Dechter（1990b）引入 CSP 文献，并由 Bacchus 和 van Beek（1998）进一步阐述。CSP 中对解的偏好在优化文献中被广泛研究；参见 Bistarelli **et al.**（1997）对将偏好纳入 CSP 框架的一般化。Dechter（1999）提出的桶消除算法也可应用于优化问题。




-   [传播方法](https://wiki.example.org/propagation_methods)在 Waltz（1975）对计算机视觉中的多面体线标记问题的成功之后变得流行。Waltz 表明，在许多问题中，传播完全消除了回溯的需求。Montanari（1974）引入了约束网络的概念以及通过路径一致性进行传播。Alan Mackworth（1977）提出了 AC-3 算法，用于强制弧一致性，并提出了结合回溯与一定程度的约束强制的一般思想。Mohr 和 Henderson（1986）开发了更高效的弧一致性算法 AC-4。Mackworth 的论文发表后不久，研究人员开始实验研究一致性强制的成本与搜索减少带来的好处之间的权衡。Haralick 和 Elliot（1980）倾向于 McGregor（1979）描述的最小前向检查算法，而 Gaschnig（1979）建议在每次变量赋值后进行全弧一致性检查——该算法后来被称为 MAC（Sabin 和 Freuder，1994）。后者论文提供了一些令人信服的证据，表明在更难的约束满足问题（CSP）中，全弧一致性检查是有效的。Freuder（1978, 1982）研究了 *k*-一致性的概念及其与解决 CSP 复杂性的关系。Apt（1999）描述了一个通用的算法框架，用于分析一致性传播算法，Bessière（2006）提供了当前的调查。

-   专门处理高阶或全局约束的方法最初是在 `constraint logic programming` 的语境中发展起来的。Marriott 和 Stuckey（1998）对该领域的研究提供了极好的覆盖。`Alldiff` 约束由 Regin（1994）、Stergiou 和 Walsh（1999）以及 van Hoeve（2001）研究。Van Hentenryck `et al.`（1998）将范围约束纳入 constraint logic programming。van Hoeve 和 Katriel（2006）提供了全局约束的调查。

-   数独已成为最广为人知的 CSP，Simonis（2005）将其描述为如此。Agerbeck 和 Hansen（2008）描述了一些策略，并表明在 *n*² × *n*² 棋盘上的数独属于 *NP*-难问题类。Reeson **et al.**（2007）展示了一个基于 CSP 技术的交互求解器。

-   回溯搜索的思想可以追溯到 Golomb 和 Baumert（1965），而它在约束满足中的应用则归功于 Bitner 和 Reingold（1975），尽管他们追溯到 19 世纪的基本算法。Bitner 和 Reingold 还引入了 MRV 启发式，他们称之为 `most-constrained-variable` 启发式。Brelaz（1979）在应用 MRV 启发式后，使用度启发式作为 tiebreaker。尽管该算法简单，它仍然是 *k*-着色任意图的最佳方法。Haralick 和 Elliot（1980）提出了最小约束值启发式。

-   基本回溯跳跃方法归功于 John Gaschnig（1977, 1979）。Kondrak 和 van Beek（1997）表明，该算法本质上被前向检查所涵盖。冲突驱动回溯跳跃由 Prosser（1993）发明。最通用且强大的智能回溯形式实际上很早就由 Stallman 和 Sussman（1977）开发。他们的 `dependency-directed backtracking` 技术促成了 `truth maintenance systems`（Doyle，1979）的发展，我们将在 Section 12.6.2 中讨论。两者之间的联系由 de Kleer（1989）分析。

* * *

`DEPENDENCY-DIRECTED BACKTRACKING`

* * *

-   Stallman 和 Sussman 的工作还引入了 `constraint learning` 的概念，即在搜索过程中获得的部分结果可以保存并稍后在搜索中重用。该概念在 Dechter（1990a）中被正式化。`Backmarking`（Gaschnig，1979）是一种简单的方法，它保存一致的和不一致的二元赋值，并利用它们避免重新检查约束。Backmarking 可以与 conflict-directed backjumping 结合；Kondrak 和 van Beek（1997）展示了一种混合算法，它在单独使用任一方法时都能够证明其优势。`dynamic backtracking`（Ginsberg，1993）的方法在回溯早期的选择不导致后续成功时，会保留后期子集变量的成功部分赋值。

* * *

`BACKMARKING`

* * *

* * *

`DYNAMIC BACKTRACKING`

* * *

-   多个随机回溯方法的经验研究由 Gomes `et al.`（2000）和 Gomes 和 Selman（2001）进行。Van Beek（2006）对回溯进行了调查。

-   约束满足问题的局部搜索由 Kirkpatrick `et al.`（1983）的模拟退火工作推广，这是一种广泛用于调度问题的方法（见 Chapter 4）。`minconflicts` 启发式最初由 Gu（1989）提出，并独立由 Minton `et al.`（1992）开发。Sosic 和 Gu（1994）展示了如何将其应用于在不到一分钟内解决 3,000,000 皇后问题。局部搜索在 *n*-皇后问题上的惊人成功促使人们重新评估“容易”和“难”问题的本质及普遍性。Peter Cheeseman `et al.`（1991）探讨了随机生成的 CSP 的难度，发现几乎所有这类问题要么是平凡容易，要么无解。只有当问题生成器的参数设置在一个狭窄范围内——大约一半的问题可求解——才会出现“难”实例。我们将在 Chapter 7 中进一步讨论这一现象。Konolige（1994）表明，在一定程度的局部结构问题上，局部搜索劣于回溯搜索，这促使了局部搜索与推理相结合的工作，如 Pinkas 和 Dechter（1995）所作。Hoos 和 Tsang（2006）调查了局部搜索技术。

-   研究 CSP 结构与复杂性的工作始于 Freuder（1985），他表明在弧一致的树上进行搜索无需任何回溯。类似的结果，并扩展到非循环超图，在数据库社区中被 Beeri `et al.`（1983）发展。Bayardo 和 Miranker（1994）展示了一种针对树结构 CSP 的算法，该算法在无需任何预处理的情况下运行线性时间。

-   自这些论文发表以来，开发更一般的结果——将解决 CSP 的复杂性与其约束图结构联系起来——取得了巨大进展。树宽的概念由图论家 Robertson 和 Seymour（1986）引入。Dechter 和 Pearl（1987, 1989）在 Freuder 的工作基础上，引入了所谓的 `induced width` 概念，并发展了在 Section 6.5 中概述的树分解方法。基于此工作以及数据库理论的结果，Gottlob `et al.`（1999a, 1999b）提出了 `hypertree width` 概念，该概念基于将 CSP 视为超图的特征。除了表明任何树宽为 w 的 CSP 可以在时间 *O*(*n*^(*w*+1) log *n*) 内求解外，他们还证明树宽 subsumes 所有先前定义的“宽度”度量，即存在情况树宽有界而其他度量无界。

-   对回溯时的回看方法重新兴趣是由 Bayardo 和 Schrag（1997）的工作激发的，他们的 RELSAT 算法结合了约束学习和回溯跳跃，并被证明优于当时的许多其他算法。这促使 Dechter 和 Mateescu（2007）开发适用于 CSP 和概率推理的 AND/OR 搜索算法。Brown `et al.`（1988）引入了 CSP 中的对称性破坏概念，Gent `et al.`（2006）给出了最近的调查。




# 变量域 `distributed constraint satisfaction` 研究在存在若干代理人时如何解决 CSP，每个代理人控制一部分约束变量。自 2000 年以来，每年都有针对该问题的工作坊，并且在其他地方也有良好覆盖 (`Collin et al.`, 1999; `Pearce et al.`, 2008; Shoham and Leyton-Brown, 2009)。

## DISTRIBUTED CONSTRAINT SATISFACTION

比较 CSP 算法主要是经验科学：很少有理论结果表明一种算法在所有问题上都优于另一种算法；相反，我们需要运行实验来观察哪些算法在典型问题实例上表现更好。正如 `Hooker` (1995) 所指出，我们需要小心区分竞争性测试——即基于运行时间进行的算法竞争——和科学测试，后者的目标是识别决定算法在一类问题上有效性的属性。

最近由 `Apt` (2003) 和 `Dechter` (2003) 编写的教材，以及 `Rossi et al.` (2006) 编辑的合集，都是 constraint processing 的优秀资源。还有若干优秀的早期综述，包括 `Kumar` (1992)、`Dechter` 与 `Frost` (2002) 以及 `Bartak` (2001) 的调查；以及 `Dechter` (1992) 与 `Mackworth` (1992) 的百科条目。`Pearson` 与 `Jeavons` (1997) 调查可解类别的 CSP，涵盖结构分解方法以及基于域或约束本身属性的方法。`Kondrak` 与 `van Beek` (1997) 提供回溯搜索算法的分析调查，而 `Bacchus` 与 `van Run` (1995) 则提供经验性调查。约束编程在 `Apt` (2003) 与 `Fruhwirth` 与 `Abdennadher` (2003) 的书中有所覆盖。若干有趣的应用在 `Freuder` 与 `Mackworth` (1994) 编辑的合集中描述。关于约束满足的论文定期发表在 *Artificial Intelligence* 和专业期刊 *Constraints* 上。主要会议场所是国际约束编程原理与实践国际会议（CP）。

### EXERCISES

**6.1** 图 6.1 中地图彩色问题有多少种解？如果允许四种颜色，解的数量是多少？两种颜色？

**6.2** 考虑在 *n* × *n* 棋盘上放置 *k* 只骑士，使得没有两只骑士相互攻击的问题，其中 *k* 已给出且 *k* ≤ *n*²。

> **a.** 选择一个 CSP 形式化。在你的形式化中，变量是什么？

> **b.** 每个变量的可能取值是什么？

> **c.** 哪些变量集合受到约束，以及如何约束？

> **d.** 现在考虑在棋盘上尽可能多地放置骑士而不产生攻击的问题。说明如何通过定义合适的 `ACTIONS` 和 `RESULT` 函数以及合理的目标函数来使用局部搜索求解。

**6.3** 考虑构建（而非求解）十字词谜题的问题：将单词填入矩形网格中。网格给定了哪些格子是空白、哪些是阴影。假设提供了单词列表（词典），任务是使用词典中的任意子集填充空白格。请以两种方式精确形式化此问题：

> **a.** 作为一般搜索问题。选择合适的搜索算法并指定启发式函数。是逐个填充字母还是逐个填充单词更好？

> **b.** 作为约束满足问题。变量是单词还是字母？

哪种形式化更好？为什么？

**6.4** 请精确地将以下每项转化为约束满足问题：

> **a.** 矩形地板规划：在大矩形中找到不重叠的位置，放置若干小矩形。

> **b.** 课程调度：有固定数量的教授和教室，以及一系列课程和可能的时间段。每位教授拥有可以教的课程集合。

> **c.** 汉密尔顿环：给定一个城市网络，选择一条访问所有城市的顺序，且不重复任何城市。

**6.5** 手动求解图 6.2 中的算术谜题，使用回溯与前向检查以及最小剩余值（MRV）和最少约束值（least-constraining-value）启发式。

**6.6** 展示如何将单个三元约束 “*A* + *B* = *C*” 转换为三个二元约束，使用辅助变量（假设域是有限的）。提示：考虑一个新变量表示其他值的对，以及诸如“*X* 是对 *Y* 的第一个元素”之类的约束。然后说明如何处理包含超过三个变量的约束，以及如何通过修改变量域消除一元约束，从而证明任何 CSP 都可以转化为仅包含二元约束的 CSP。

**6.7** 考虑下面的逻辑谜题：在五栋不同颜色的房子中，居住着五位不同国籍、偏好不同糖果品牌、饮料和宠物的五个人。根据给出的事实，回答“斑马住在哪栋房子，以及它喝什么水？”

> 英国人住在红色房子里。

> 西班牙人养狗。

> 诺尔人住在最左边的第一栋房子。

> 绿色房子紧挨着象牙色房子。

> 吃赫歇巧克力的人住在与养狐狸的人住的房子相邻。

> 奇巧（Kit Kats）在黄色房子里吃。

> 诺尔人住在蓝色房子旁边。

> 斯玛特 ies（Smarties）爱好者养 ail（snails）。

> 斯尼克（Snickers）爱好者喝橙汁。

> 乌克兰人喝茶。

> 日本人吃 Milky Ways。

> 奇巧（Kit Kats）在与养马的房子相邻。

> 咖啡在绿色房子里喝。

> 牛奶在中间房子里喝。

讨论不同的 CSP 表示方式。为什么会有人偏好某一种表示方式而不是另一种？

**6.8** 给定包含 8 个节点 *A*[1]、*A*[2]、*A*[3]、*A*[4]、*H*、*T*、*F*[1]、*F*[2] 的图，其中 *A[i]* 与 *A*[*i*+1] 相连，每个 *A[i]* 还与 *H* 相连，*H* 与 *T* 相连，*T* 与每个 *F[i]* 相连。请手动使用以下策略求出 3 色彩的图染色：后退搜索结合冲突导向回退（conflict-directed backjumping），变量顺序 *A*[1], *H*, *A*[4], *F*[1], *A*[2], *F*[2], *A*[3], *T*, *A*[3]，值顺序 *R*, *G*, *B*。

-   [> *在本节中，我们设计能够形成复杂世界表示的代理，使用推理过程推导新的世界表示，并利用这些新表示来推断应做什么。*

-   [人类似乎知道一些事；而这些知识帮助他们做事。这些并非空洞的陈述。它们对人类智能的实现提出了强有力的主张——不是由纯粹的 reflex 机制实现，而是通过作用于内部**知识表示**的**推理**过程实现。在 AI 中，这种对智能的处理方式体现在**知识基础代理**中。]

-   [* * *]

-   [REASONING]

-   [* * *]

-   [KNOWLEDGE-BASED AGENTS]

-   [* * *]

-   [The central component of a knowledge-based agent is its **knowledge base**, or KB. A knowledge base is a set of **sentences**. (Here “sentence” is used as a technical term. It is related but not identical to the sentences of English and other natural languages.) Each sentence is expressed in a language called a **knowledge representation language** and represents some assertion about the world. Sometimes we dignify a sentence with the name **axiom**, when the sentence is taken as given without being derived from other sentences.]

-   [* * *]

-   [KNOWLEDGE BASE]

-   [* * *]

-   [* * *]

-   [SENTENCE]

-   [* * *]

-   [* * *]

-   [KNOWLEDGE REPRESENTATION LANGUAGE]

-   [* * *]

-   [AXIOM]

-   [* * *]

-   [There must be a way to add new sentences to the knowledge base and a way to query what is known. The standard names for these operations are TELL and ASK, respectively. Both operations may involve **inference**—that is, deriving new sentences from old. Inference must obey the requirement that when one ASKs a question of the knowledge base, the answer should follow from what has been told (or TELLed) to the knowledge base previously. Later in this chapter, we will be more precise about the crucial word “follow.” For now, take it to mean that the inference process should not make things up as it goes along.]

-   [* * *]

-   [INFERENCE]

-   [Figure 7.1 shows the outline of a knowledge-based agent program. Like all our agents, it takes a percept as input and returns an action. The agent maintains a knowledge base, *KB*, which may initially contain some **background knowledge**.]

-   [* * *]

-   [BACKGROUND KNOWLEDGE]

-   [* * *]

-   [Each time the agent program is called, it does three things. First, it TELLS the knowledge base what it perceives. Second, it ASKs the knowledge base what action it should perform. In the process of answering this query, extensive reasoning may be done about the current state of the world, about the outcomes of possible action sequences, and so on. Third, the agent program TELLS the knowledge base which action was chosen, and the agent executes the action.]



The details of the representation language are hidden inside three functions that implement the interface between the sensors and actuators on one side and the core representation and reasoning system on the other. `MAKE-PERCEPT-SENTENCE` constructs a sentence asserting that the agent perceived the given percept at the given time. `MAKE-ACTION-QUERY` constructs a sentence that asks what action should be done at the current time. Finally, `MAKE-ACTION-SENTENCE` constructs a sentence asserting that the chosen action was executed. The details of the inference mechanisms are hidden inside `TELL` and `ASK`. Later sections will reveal these details.

The agent in `Figure 7.1` appears quite similar to the agents with internal state described in `Chapter 2`. Because of the definitions of `TELL` and `ASK`, however, the knowledge-based agent is not an arbitrary program for calculating actions. It is amenable to a description at the `knowledge level`, where we need specify only what the agent knows and what its goals are, in order to fix its behavior. For example, an automated taxi might have the goal of taking a passenger from San Francisco to Marin County and might know that the Golden Gate Bridge is the only link between the two locations. Then we can expect it to cross the Golden Gate Bridge `because it knows that that will achieve its goal`. Notice that this analysis is independent of how the taxi works at the `implementation level`. It doesn’t matter whether its geographical knowledge is implemented as linked lists or pixel maps, or whether it reasons by manipulating strings of symbols stored in registers or by propagating noisy signals in a network of neurons.

* * *

`KNOWLEDGE LEVEL`

* * *

* * *

`IMPLEMENTATION LEVEL`

* * *

> ![image](img/00169.jpg)

> **Figure 7.1** A generic knowledge-based agent. Given a percept, the agent adds the percept to its knowledge base, asks the knowledge base for the best action, and tells the knowledge base that it has in fact taken that action.

A knowledge-based agent can be built simply by `TELL`ing it what it needs to know. Starting with an empty knowledge base, the agent designer can `TELL` sentences one by one until the agent knows how to operate in its environment. This is called the `declarative` approach to system building. In contrast, the `procedural` approach encodes desired behaviors directly as program code. In the 1970s and 1980s, advocates of the two approaches engaged in heated debates. We now understand that a successful agent often combines both declarative and procedural elements in its design, and that declarative knowledge can often be compiled into more efficient procedural code.

* * *

`DECLARATIVE`

* * *

We can also provide a knowledge-based agent with mechanisms that allow it to learn for itself. These mechanisms, which are discussed in `Chapter 18`, create general knowledge about the environment from a series of percepts. A learning agent can be fully autonomous.

### 7.2 THE WUMPUS WORLD

In this section we describe an environment in which knowledge-based agents can show their worth. The **wumpus world** is a cave consisting of rooms connected by passageways. Lurking somewhere in the cave is the terrible wumpus, a beast that eats anyone who enters its room. The wumpus can be shot by an agent, but the agent has only one arrow. Some rooms contain bottomless pits that will trap anyone who wanders into these rooms (except for the wumpus, which is too big to fall in). The only mitigating feature of this bleak environment is the possibility of finding a heap of gold. Although the wumpus world is rather tame by modern computer game standards, it illustrates some important points about intelligence.

* * *

`WUMPUS WORLD`

* * *

A sample wumpus world is shown in `Figure 7.2`. The precise definition of the task environment is given, as suggested in `Section 2.3`, by the PEAS description:

> • **Performance measure**: +1000 for climbing out of the cave with the gold, −1000 for falling into a pit or being eaten by the wumpus, −1 for each action taken and −10 for using up the arrow. The game ends either when the agent dies or when the agent climbs out of the cave.

> • **Environment**: A 4 × 4 grid of rooms. The agent always starts in the square labeled `[1,1]`, facing to the right. The locations of the gold and the wumpus are chosen randomly, with a uniform distribution, from the squares other than the start square. In addition, each square other than the start can be a pit, with probability 0.2.

> • **Actuators**: The agent can move *Forward*, *TurnLeft* by 90°, or *TurnRight* by 90^°. The agent dies a miserable death if it enters a square containing a pit or a live wumpus. (It is safe, albeit smelly, to enter a square with a dead wumpus.) If an agent tries to move forward and bumps into a wall, then the agent does not move. The action *Grab* can be used to pick up the gold if it is in the same square as the agent. The action *Shoot* can be used to fire an arrow in a straight line in the direction the agent is facing. The arrow continues until it either hits (and hence kills) the wumpus or hits a wall. The agent has only one arrow, so only the first *Shoot* action has any effect. Finally, the action *Climb* can be used to climb out of the cave, but only from square `[1,1]`.

> • **Sensors**: The agent has five sensors, each of which gives a single bit of information:

> **–** In the square containing the wumpus and in the directly (not diagonally) adjacent squares, the agent will perceive a *Stench*.

> **–** In the squares directly adjacent to a pit, the agent will perceive a *Breeze*.

> **–** In the square where the gold is, the agent will perceive a *Glitter*.

> **–** When an agent walks into a wall, it will perceive a *Bump*.

> **–** When the wumpus is killed, it emits a woeful *Scream* that can be perceived anywhere in the cave.

> The percepts will be given to the agent program in the form of a list of five symbols; for example, if there is a stench and a breeze, but no glitter, bump, or scream, the agent program will get `["Stench", "Breeze", "None", "None", "None"]`.

We can characterize the wumpus environment along the various dimensions given in `Chapter 2`. Clearly, it is discrete, static, and single-agent. (The wumpus doesn’t move, fortunately.) It is sequential, because rewards may come only after many actions are taken. It is partially observable, because some aspects of the state are not directly perceivable: the agent’s location, the wumpus’s state of health, and the availability of an arrow. As for the locations of the pits and the wumpus: we could treat them as unobserved parts of the state that happen to be immutable—in which case, the transition model for the environment is completely known; or we could say that the transition model itself is unknown because the agent doesn’t know which *Forward* actions are fatal–in which case, discovering the locations of pits and wumpus completes the agent’s knowledge of the transition model.

> ![image](img/00170.jpg)

> **Figure 7.2** A typical wumpus world. The agent is in the bottom left corner, facing right.

For an agent in the environment, the main challenge is its initial ignorance of the configuration of the environment; overcoming this ignorance seems to require logical reasoning. In most instances of the wumpus world, it is possible for the agent to retrieve the gold safely. Occasionally, the agent must choose between going home empty-handed and risking death to find the gold. About 21% of the environments are utterly unfair, because the gold is in a pit or surrounded by pits.




让我们观察一个基于知识的 wumpus 代理人探索如图 7.2 所示的环境。我们使用一种非正式的知识表示语言，它由在网格中写下符号组成（如在图 7.3 和图 7.4 中所示）。

代理人的初始知识库包含环境的规则，如前所述；特别是，它知道自己位于[1,1]，且[1,1]是安全的方格；我们用字母“A”和“OK”分别在方格[1,1]中表示这一点。

第一个感知是[*None, None, None, None, None*]，从中代理人可以推断出其相邻方格[1,2]和[2,1]没有危险——它们是 OK 的。图 7.3（a）显示了代理人在此时的知识状态。

谨慎的代理人只会进入它知道是 OK 的方格。假设代理人决定向前移动到[2,1]。代理人在[2,1]感知到微风（用“B”表示），因此一定有一个坑在相邻方格中。根据游戏规则，坑不可能在[1,1]，所以坑一定在[2,2]、[3,1]或两者之间。图 7.3（b）中的“P?”符号表示这些方格中可能存在坑。此时，只有一个已知的 OK 方格且尚未被访问。因此，谨慎的代理人会转身返回[1,1]，然后前往[1,2]。

代理人在[1,2]感知到恶臭，从而得到如图 7.4（a）所示的知识状态。[1,2]的恶臭意味着附近一定有 wumpus。但根据游戏规则，wumpus 不可能在[1,1]，也不能在[2,2]（否则代理人在[2,1]时会感知到恶臭）。因此，代理人可以推断 wumpus 位于[1,3]。符号“W!”表示这一推断。此外，[1,2]缺乏微风意味着[2,2]中没有坑。然而，代理人已经推断出坑一定在[2,2]或[3,1]之间，因此这意味着坑必须在[3,1]。这是一个相当困难的推断，因为它结合了在不同时间、不同地点获得的知识，并依赖于缺乏感知来完成关键一步。

> ![image](img/00171.jpg)

> **Figure 7.3** 代理人在 wumpus 世界中所做的第一步。（a）初始情况，在感知[*None, None, None, None, None*]之后。（b）移动一次后，感知[*None, Breeze, None, None, None*]。

> ![image](img/00172.jpg)

> **Figure 7.4** 代理人进展的两个后期阶段。（a）第三步之后，感知[*Stench, None, None, None, None*]。（b）第五步之后，感知[*Stench, Breeze, Glitter, None, None*]。

代理人现在证明自己认为[2,2]中既没有坑也没有 wumpus，因此安全可以前往那里。我们没有展示代理人在[2,2]的知识状态；我们只是假设代理人转身并移动到[2,3]，从而得到图 7.4（b）。在[2,3]，代理人检测到闪光，因此应该捡起金条然后回家。

请注意，在每种代理人根据现有信息得出结论的情况中，该结论如果现有信息正确，则是*保证*正确的。这是逻辑推理的一个根本属性。在本章余下部分，我们描述如何构建能够表示信息并像前文所述那样得出结论的逻辑代理。

### 7.3 LOGIC

本节总结了逻辑表示和推理的基本概念。这些美妙的思想与逻辑的任何特定形式无关，因此我们将在下一节中推迟对这些形式的技术细节，转而使用熟悉的普通算术例子。

在`Section 7.1`中，我们指出知识库由句子组成。这些句子是根据表示语言的**语法**来表达的，语法规定了所有 well‑formed 句子。语法概念在普通算术中很明确：“*x* + *y* = 4”是一个 well‑formed 句子，而“*x*4*y*\+ =”不是。

* * *

SYNTAX

* * *

一个逻辑必须同时定义句子的**语义**或含义。语义定义了每个句子相对于每个**可能世界**的**真值**。例如，算术的语义规定，句子“*x* + *y* = 4”在* x* 为 2 且* y* 为 2 的世界中为真，而在* x* 为 1 且* y* 为 1 的世界中为假。在标准逻辑中，每个句子在每个可能世界中要么真要么假——不存在介于两者之间的情况。¹

* * *

SEMANTICS

* * *

* * *

TRUTH

* * *

* * *

POSSIBLE WORLD

* * *

当我们需要精确时，我们使用**模型**一词代替“可能世界”。而“可能世界”可能被认为是（潜在的）代理可能在的环境，而模型是数学抽象，每个模型仅固定相关句子的真假。我们可以非正式地把可能世界想像成，例如，*x* 个男人和 *y* 个女人坐在桌边玩桥牌，而句子 *x* + *y* = 4 在总人数为四时为真。形式上，可能模型就是所有给变量 *x* 和 *y* 分配实数的可能赋值。每个这样的赋值确定了包含变量 *x* 和 *y* 的算术句子的真值。如果句子 α 在模型 m 中为真，我们说 m **满足** α，或者 m **是** α 的**模型**。我们使用符号 *M*(α) 表示所有 α 的模型的集合。

* * *

MODEL

* * *

* * *

SATISFACTION

* * *

* * *

ENTAILMENT

* * *

* * *

现在我们有了真值的概念，便可以讨论逻辑推理。这涉及句子之间的**逻辑蕴含**关系——即一个句子*跟随逻辑地*从另一个句子推导出来的思想。在数学记号中，我们写

> α ⊨ β

> ![image](img/00173.jpg)

> **Figure 7.5** 关于方格[1,2]、[2,2]和[3,1]中坑的存在的可能模型。实线显示对应于观察到[1,1]没有任何事项且[2,1]有微风的知识库（KB）。（a）虚线显示 α[1]（[1,2]中无坑）的模型。（b）虚线显示 α[2]（[2,2]中无坑）的模型。

来表示 α 蕴含 β。逻辑蕴含的正式定义是：α ⊨ β 当且仅当，在所有 α 为真 的模型中，β 也为真。用刚才引入的记号，我们可以写

> α ⊨ β if and only if *M*(α) ⊆ *M*(β).

（注意这里的 ⊆ 方向：如果 α ⊨ β，则 α 是 *更强* 的断言：它排除了更多的可能世界。）蕴含关系在算术中很熟悉；我们乐于接受这样的想法：句子 *x* = 0 蕴含句子 *xy* = 0。显然，在任何* x* 为零的模型中，无论* y* 取何值，*xy* 都为零。

我们可以将同样的分析应用于前文 wumpus‑world 推理示例中。考虑图 7.3（b）所示的情形：代理人在[1,1]没有感知到任何事项，而在[2,1]感知到微风。这些感知，结合代理人对 wumpus 世界规则的知识，构成了 KB。代理人关心（among other things）相邻方格[1,2]、[2,2]和[3,1]是否含有坑。每个方格可能有坑也可能没有，因此（为了本例的目的）有 2³ = 8 种可能模型。这些八种模型在图 7.5 中显示。²



The KB 可以被视为一个句子集合，或者一个主张所有单个句子的单一句子。KB 在与代理人已知事实相矛盾的模型中为假；例如，KB 在任何模型中为假，其中 \[1,2\] 包含一个坑，因为 \[1,1\] 中没有微风。事实上，KB 为真的模型只有三个，这些模型由实线在 Figure 7.5 周围标示。现在让我们考虑两个可能的结论：

> ![image](img/00174.jpg)

我们在图 7.5 中分别用虚线将 α[1] 和 α[2] 的模型包围在 7.5(*a*) 和 7.5(*b*) 中。通过观察，我们可以看到：

> 在所有 KB 为真的模型中，α[1] 也为真。

因此，KB ⊨ α[1]：\[1,2\] 中没有坑。我们也可以看到：

> 在 KB 为真的某些模型中，α[2] 为假。

因此，![image](img/00175.jpg)：代理人 *不能* 推断 \[2,2\] 中没有坑。（它也不能推断 \[2,2\] 中有坑。）

The preceding example not only illustrates entailment but also shows how the definition of entailment can be applied to derive conclusions—that is, to carry out **logical inference**. The inference algorithm illustrated in Figure 7.5 is called **model checking**, because it enumerates all possible models to check that α is true in all models in which *KB* is true, that is, that *M*(*KB*) ⊆ *M*(α).

* * *

LOGICAL INFERENCE

* * *

* * *

MODEL CHECKING

* * *

In understanding entailment and inference, it might help to think of the set of all consequences of *KB* as a haystack and of α as a needle. Entailment is like the needle being in the haystack; inference is like finding it. This distinction is embodied in some formal notation: if an inference algorithm i can derive α from *KB*, we write

> ![image](img/00176.jpg)

which is pronounced “α is derived from *KB* by *i*” or “*i* derives α from *KB*.”

An inference algorithm that derives only entailed sentences is called **sound** or **truth-preserving**. Soundness is a highly desirable property. An unsound inference procedure essentially makes things up as it goes along—it announces the discovery of nonexistent needles. It is easy to see that model checking, when it is applicable,⁴ is a sound procedure.

* * *

SOUND

* * *

* * *

TRUTH-PRESERVING

* * *

The property of **completeness** is also desirable: an inference algorithm is complete if it can derive any sentence that is entailed. For real haystacks, which are finite in extent, it seems obvious that a systematic examination can always decide whether the needle is in the haystack. For many knowledge bases, however, the haystack of consequences is infinite, and completeness becomes an important issue.⁵ Fortunately, there are complete inference procedures for logics that are sufficiently expressive to handle many knowledge bases.

* * *

COMPLETENESS

* * *

![hand](img/00006.jpg)

We have described a reasoning process whose conclusions are guaranteed to be true in any world in which the premises are true; in particular, *if KB is true in the* real *world, then any sentence α derived from KB by a sound inference procedure is also true in the real world.* So, while an inference process operates on “syntax”—internal physical configurations such as bits in registers or patterns of electrical blips in brains—the process *corresponds* to the real-world relationship whereby some aspect of the real world is the case⁶ by virtue of other aspects of the real world being the case. This correspondence between world and representation is illustrated in Figure 7.6.

> ![image](img/00177.jpg)

> **Figure 7.6** Sentences are physical configurations of the agent, and reasoning is a process of constructing new physical configurations from old ones. Logical reasoning should ensure that the new configurations represent aspects of the world that actually follow from the aspects that the old configurations represent.

The final issue to consider is **grounding**—the connection between logical reasoning processes and the real environment in which the agent exists. In particular, *how do we know that KB is true in the real world?* (After all, *KB* is just “syntax” inside the agent’s head.) This is a philosophical question about which many, many books have been written. (See Chapter 26.) A simple answer is that the agent’s sensors create the connection. For example, our wumpus-world agent has a smell sensor. The agent program creates a suitable sentence whenever there is a smell. Then, whenever that sentence is in the knowledge base, it is true in the real world. Thus, the meaning and truth of percept sentences are defined by the processes of sensing and sentence construction that produce them. What about the rest of the agent’s knowledge, such as its belief that wumpuses cause smells in adjacent squares? This is not a direct representation of a single percept, but a general rule—derived, perhaps, from perceptual experience but not identical to a statement of that experience. General rules like this are produced by a sentence construction process called **learning**, which is the subject of Part V. Learning is fallible. It could be the case that wumpuses cause smells *except on February 29 in leap years*, which is when they take their baths. Thus, *KB* may not be true in the real world, but with good learning procedures, there is reason for optimism.

* * *

GROUNDING

* * *

### 7.4 PROPOSITIONAL LOGIC: A VERY SIMPLE LOGIC

We now present a simple but powerful logic called **propositional logic**. We cover the syntax of propositional logic and its semantics—the way in which the truth of sentences is determined. Then we look at **entailment**—the relation between a sentence and another sentence that follows from it—and see how this leads to a simple algorithm for logical inference. Everything takes place, of course, in the wumpus world.

* * *

PROPOSITIONAL LOGIC

* * *

#### **7.4.1 Syntax**

The **syntax** of propositional logic defines the allowable sentences. The **atomic sentences** consist of a single **proposition symbol**. Each such symbol stands for a proposition that can be true or false. We use symbols that start with an uppercase letter and may contain other letters or subscripts, for example: *P*, *Q*, *R*, *W*[1,3] and *North*. The names are arbitrary but are often chosen to have some mnemonic value—we use *W*[1,3] to stand for the proposition that the wumpus is in \[1,3\]. (Remember that symbols such as *W*[1,3] are *atomic*, i.e., *W*, 1, and 3 are not meaningful parts of the symbol.) There are two proposition symbols with fixed meanings: *True* is the always-true proposition and *False* is the always-false proposition. **Complex sentences** are constructed from simpler sentences, using parentheses and **logical connectives**. There are five connectives in common use:

* * *

ATOMIC SENTENCES

* * *

* * *

PROPOSITION SYMBOL

* * *

* * *

COMPLEX SENTENCES

* * *

* * *

LOGICAL CONNECTIVES

* * *

> ¬ (not). A sentence such as ¬*W*[1,3] is called the **negation** of *W*[1,3]. A **literal** is either an atomic sentence (a **positive literal**) or a negated atomic sentence (a **negative literal**).

* * *

NEGATION

* * *

* * *

LITERAL

* * *

> ⋀ (and). A sentence whose main connective is ⋀, such as *W*[1,3] ⋀ *P*[3,1], is called a **conjunction**; its parts are the **conjuncts**. (The ⋀ looks like an “A” for “And.”)

* * *

CONJUNCTION

* * *




> ⋁ (or). 一个使用 ⋁ 的句子，例如 (*W*[1,3] ⋀ *P*[3,1]) ⋁ *W*[2,2]，是**析取**的**析取式**，其中 (*W*[1,3] ⋀ *P*[3,1]) 和 *W*[2,2] 是**析取式的** **析取元**。（历史上，⋁ 源自拉丁语 “vel”，意为 “或”。对大多数人来说，记住 ⋁ 为倒置的 ⋀ 更容易。）

* * *

DISJUNCTION

* * *

> ⇒ (implies). 一个句子，如 (*W*[1,3] ⋀ *P*[3,1]) ⇒ ¬*W*[2,2]，称为**蕴含**（或条件）。它的**前提**或**前件**是 (*W*[1,3] ⋀ *P*[3,1])，它的**结论**或**后件**是 ¬*W*[2,2]。蕴含也被称为**规则**或**if–then** 陈述。蕴含符号有时在其他书中写作 ⊃ 或 →。

* * *

IMPLICATION

* * *

* * *

PREMISE

* * *

* * *

CONCLUSION

* * *

* * *

RULES

* * *

> ⇔ (if and only if). 句子 *W*[1,3] ⇔ ¬*W*[2,2] 是**双条件**句。**其他书**中也写作 ≡。

* * *

BICONDITIONAL

* * *

> ![image](img/00178.jpg)

> **Figure 7.7** 从最高到最低的**命题逻辑句子**的**BNF（巴克斯–纳尔形式）语法，以及运算符优先级。

Figure 7.7 提供了命题逻辑的正式语法；如果你不熟悉 BNF 记号，请参阅 第 1060 页。BNF 语法本身是**模糊**的；包含多个运算符的句子可以通过语法在多种方式上解析。为了消除歧义，我们为每个运算符定义**优先级**。**否定**运算符（¬）优先级最高，这意味着在句子 ¬A ⋀ B 中，¬ 绑定得最紧，使其等价于 (¬A) ⋀ B 而不是 ¬(*A* ⋀ *B*)。（算术符号的写法相同：−2+4 = 2，而不是 −6。）如果有疑问，请使用括号以确保正确的解释。方括号与圆括号含义相同；选择哪种仅为了让人更易阅读句子。

#### **7.4.2 Semantics**

在指定了**命题逻辑**的**语法**后，我们现在指定其**语义**。语义定义了在特定**模型**下判断句子真假的规则。在命题逻辑中，一个**模型**仅仅为每个命题符号指定**真值**——**true** 或 **false**。例如，如果知识库中的句子使用了命题符号 *P*[1,2]、*P*[2,2] 和 *P*[3,1]，则一个可能的模型是

> *m*[1] = {*P*[1,2] = *false*, *P*[2,2] = *false*, *P*[3,1] = *true*}.

* * *

TRUTH VALUE

* * *

在三个命题符号的情况下，共有 2³ = 8 种可能的模型——正如 Figure 7.5 所描绘的那样。请注意，这些模型是**纯数学**对象，与 **wumpus 世界** 没有必然联系。*P*[1,2] 仅是一个符号；它可能表示“\[1,2\] 存在坑”，也可能表示“我今天和明天在巴黎”。

**命题逻辑的语义** 必须说明如何在任意模型下计算任意句子的真值。这通过**递归**实现。所有句子都由原子句子和五个**连接词**构成；因此，我们需要说明如何计算原子句子的真值，以及如何计算由每个连接词构成的句子的真值。原子句子很简单：

> • *True* 在每个模型中都为真，*False* 在每个模型中都为假。

> • 除了原子命题符号外，其他命题符号的真值必须在模型中直接指定。例如，在前面提到的模型 *m*[1] 中，*P*[1,2] 为假。

对于复杂句子，我们有五条规则，适用于任何子句 *P* 和 *Q* 在任意模型 *m*（这里 “iff” 表示 “当且仅当”）：

> • ¬*P* 为真 **当且仅当** *P* 在 *m* 中为假。

> • *P* ⋀ *Q* 为真 **当且仅当** *P* 和 *Q* 在 *m* 中均为真。

> • *P* ⋁ *Q* 为真 **当且仅当** *P* 或 *Q* 在 *m* 中至少有一个为真。

> • *P* ⇒ *Q* 在 *m* 中为假 **仅当** *P* 为真 且 *Q* 为假。

> • *P* ⇔ *Q* 为真 **当且仅当** *P* 和 *Q* 在 *m* 中均为真 或均为假。

* * *

TRUTH TABLE

* * *

规则也可以用**真值表**表示，真值表指定了复杂句子在其各组成部分的真值所有可能赋值下的真值。五个连接词的真值表在 Figure 7.8 中给出。根据这些表，我们可以在任意模型 *m* 下递归计算任意句子 *s* 的真值。例如，句子 ¬*P*[1,2] ⋀ (*P*[2,2] ⋁ *P*[3,1]) 在 *m*[1] 中的评估结果为 true ⋀ (*false* ⋁ *true*) = *true* ⋀ *true* = *true*。Exercise 7.3 要求你编写算法 PL-TRUE?(*s*, *m*)，该算法计算命题逻辑句子 *s* 在模型 *m* 中的真值。

> ![image](img/00179.jpg)

> **Figure 7.8** 真值表为五个逻辑连接词。例如，要计算当 *P* 为真、*Q* 为假时 *P* ⋁ *Q* 的值，先在左侧找到 *P* 为真且 *Q* 为假的那一行（第三行），然后在该行对应的 *P*⋁*Q* 列中查找结果：true。

真值表中 “且”、“或” 和 “否定” 的真值表与我们对英语单词 “and”, “or”, “not” 的直觉相符。主要的混淆点在于 *P* ⋁ *Q* 在 *P* 为真 或 *Q* 为真（甚至两者都为真）时为真。另一种连接词 “exclusive or” (“xor” 缩写) 在两个析取式均为真时返回假。关于独占或的符号没有共识，一些选择包括 ⋁、≠ 或 ⊕。

**蕴含**的真值表可能不符合人们对 “*P* implies *Q*” 或 “如果 *P* 则 *Q*" 的直观理解。一方面，命题逻辑不要求 *P* 和 *Q* 之间存在因果关系或关联。例如，“5 是奇数 implies 东京是日本的首都” 在命题逻辑中是一个真句子（在常规解释下），尽管在英语中显得很奇怪。另一个混淆点是，只要前件为假，任何蕴含句都是真。例如，“5 是偶数 implies Sam 是聪明” 是真，无论 Sam 是否聪明。这看似荒谬，但如果把 “*P* ⇒ *Q*" 理解为 “如果 *P* 为真，我声称 *Q* 为真；否则我不声称任何内容”，那么只有当 *P* 为真 且 *Q* 为假时，该句才为假。

**双条件** *P* ⇔ *Q* 为真 **当且仅当** *P* ⇒ *Q* 和 *Q* ⇒ *P* 都为真。在英语中常写作 “*P* if and only if *Q*”。许多 **wumpus 世界** 的规则最好用 ⇔ 表示。例如，一个方格是 **清爽** *if* 一个相邻方格有坑，且一个方格是 **清爽** *only if* 一个相邻方格有坑。因此我们需要双条件：

> *B*[1,1] ⇔ (*P*[1,2] ⋁ *P*[2,1]),

其中 *B*[1,1] 表示 \[1,1\] 存在微风。

#### **7.4.3 A simple knowledge base**

在定义了命题逻辑的语义后，我们可以为 **wumpus 世界** 构建一个**知识库**。我们首先关注 **不可变** 的方面，mutable 部分稍后再讨论。目前，我们需要为每个 \[*x*, *y*\] 位置定义以下符号：

> *P*[*x,y*] 为真 当且仅当 \[*x, y*\] 存在坑。

> *W[x,y]* 为真 当且仅当 \[*x, y*\] 存在 **wumpus**（死或活）。

> *B*[*x,y*] 为真 当且仅当 代理在 \[*x, y*\] 感知到微风。

> *S[x,y]* 为真 当且仅当 代理在 \[*x, y*\] 感知到臭味。

我们编写的句子足以推导 ¬*P*[1,2]（\[1,2\] 没有坑），如在 第 7.3 节 非正式讨论中所作。我们为每个句子标记 *R*[*i*] 以便引用：

> • \[1,1\] 位置没有坑：

> *R*[1] : ¬*P*[1,1].

> • 一个方格是 **清爽** **当且仅当** 一个相邻方格存在坑。此规则必须为每个方格陈述；目前仅包括相关的方格：

> *R*[2] : *B*[1,1] ⇔ (*P*[1,2] ⋁ *P*[2,1]).

> *R*[3] : *B*[2,1] ⇔ (*P*[1,1] ⋁ *P*[2,2] ⋁ *P*[3,1]).




> • 先前的句子在所有 wumpus 世界中均为真。现在我们为代理人所在的特定世界中前两个被访问的方格加入了微风感知，从而导致如 Figure 7.3(b) 所示的情况。

> *R*[4] : ¬*B*[1,1].

> *R*[5] : *B*[2,1].

#### **7.4.4 A simple inference procedure**

Our goal now is to decide whether *KB* ⊨ α for some sentence α. For example, is ¬*P*[1,2] entailed by our *KB*? Our first algorithm for inference is a model-checking approach that is a direct implementation of the definition of entailment: enumerate the models, and check that α is true in every model in which *KB* is true. Models are assignments of true or false to every proposition symbol. Returning to our wumpus-world example, the relevant proposition symbols are *B*[1,1], *B*[2,1], *P*[1,1], *P*[1,2], *P*[2,1], *P*[2,2], and *P*[3,1]. With seven symbols, there are 2⁷ = 128 possible models; in three of these, *KB* is true (Figure 7.9). In those three models, ¬*P*[1,2] is true, hence there is no pit in \[1,2\]. On the other hand, *P*[2,2] is true in two of the three models and false in one, so we cannot yet tell whether there is a pit in \[2,2\].

Figure 7.9 reproduces in a more precise form the reasoning illustrated in Figure 7.5. A general algorithm for deciding entailment in propositional logic is shown in Figure 7.10. Like the BACKTRACKING-SEARCH algorithm on page 215, TT-ENTAILS? performs a recursive enumeration of a finite space of assignments to symbols. The algorithm is `sound` because it implements directly the definition of entailment, and `complete` because it works for any *KB* and α and always terminates—there are only finitely many models to examine.

Of course, “finitely many” is not always the same as “few.” If *KB* and α contain *n* symbols in all, then there are 2^(*n*) models. Thus, the time complexity of the algorithm is *O*(2^(*n*)). (The space complexity is only *O*(*n*) because the enumeration is depth-first.) Later in this chapter we show algorithms that are much more efficient in many cases. Unfortunately, propositional entailment is co-NP-complete (i.e., probably no easier than NP-complete—see Appendix A), so *every known inference algorithm for propositional logic has a worst-case complexity that is exponential in the size of the input*.

![image](img/00006.jpg)

> ![image](img/00180.jpg)

> **Figure 7.9** A truth table constructed for the knowledge base given in the text. *KB* is true if *R*[1] through *R*[5] are true, which occurs in just 3 of the 128 rows (the ones underlined in the right-hand column). In all 3 rows, *P*[1,2] is false, so there is no pit in \[1,2\]. On the other hand, there might (or might not) be a pit in \[2,2\].

> ![image](img/00181.jpg)

> **Figure 7.10** A truth-table enumeration algorithm for deciding propositional entailment. (TT stands for truth table.) PL-TRUE? returns *true* if a sentence holds within a model. The variable *model* represents a partial model—an assignment to some of the symbols. The keyword “**and**” is used here as a logical operation on its two arguments, returning *true* or *false*.

> ![image](img/00182.jpg)

> **Figure 7.11** Standard logical equivalences. The symbols α, β, and γ stand for arbitrary sentences of propositional logic.

### 7.5 PROPOSITIONAL THEOREM PROVING

So far, we have shown how to determine entailment by *model checking*: enumerating models and showing that the sentence must hold in all models. In this section, we show how entailment can be done by **theorem proving**—applying rules of inference directly to the sentences in our knowledge base to construct a proof of the desired sentence without consulting models. If the number of models is large but the length of the proof is short, then theorem proving can be more efficient than model checking.

* * *

THEOREM PROVING

* * *

Before we plunge into the details of theorem-proving algorithms, we will need some additional concepts related to entailment. The first concept is **logical equivalence**: two sentences α and β are logically equivalent if they are true in the same set of models. We write this as α ≡ β. For example, we can easily show (using truth tables) that *P* ⋀ Q and Q ⋀ *P* are logically equivalent; other equivalences are shown in Figure 7.11. These equivalences play much the same role in logic as arithmetic identities do in ordinary mathematics. An alternative definition of equivalence is as follows: any two sentences α and β are equivalent only if each of them entails the other:

* * *

LOGICAL EQUIVALENCE

* * *

> α ≡ β if and only if α ⊨ β and β ⊨ α.

The second concept we will need is **validity**. A sentence is valid if it is true in *all* models. For example, the sentence *P* ⋄ ¬*P* is valid. Valid sentences are also known as **tautologies**—they are *necessarily* true. Because the sentence *True* is true in all models, every valid sentence is logically equivalent to *True*. What good are valid sentences? From our definition of entailment, we can derive the **deduction theorem**, which was known to the ancient Greeks:

> *For any sentences* α *and* β, α ⊨ β *if and only if the sentence* (α ⇒ β) *is valid.*

* * *

VALIDITY

* * *

* * *

TAUTOLOGY

* * *

* * *

DEDUCTION THEOREM

* * *

![image](img/00006.jpg)

(Exercise 7.5 asks for a proof.) Hence, we can decide if α ⊨ β by checking that (α ⇒ β) is true in every model—which is essentially what the inference algorithm in Figure 7.10 does— or by proving that (α ⇒ β) is equivalent to True. Conversely, the deduction theorem states that every valid implication sentence describes a legitimate inference.

The final concept we will need is **satisfiability**. A sentence is satisfiable if it is true in, or satisfied by, *some* model. For example, the knowledge base given earlier, (*R*[1] ⋀ *R*[2] ⋀ *R*[3] ⋀ *R*[4] ⋀ *R*[5]), is satisfiable because there are three models in which it is true, as shown in Figure 7.9. Satisfiability can be checked by enumerating the possible models until one is found that satisfies the sentence. The problem of determining the satisfiability of sentences in propositional logic—the **SAT** problem—was the first problem proved to be NP-complete. Many problems in computer science are really satisfiability problems. For example, all the constraint satisfaction problems in Chapter 6 ask whether the constraints are satisfiable by some assignment.

* * *

SATISFIABILITY

* * *

* * *

SAT

* * *

Validity and satisfiability are of course connected: α is valid iff ¬α is unsatisfiable; contrapositively, α is satisfiable iff ¬α is not valid. We also have the following useful result:

> α ⊨ β *if and only if the sentence* (α ⋀ ¬β) *is unsatisfiable.*

Proving β from α by checking the unsatisfiability of (α ⋀ ¬β) corresponds exactly to the standard mathematical proof technique of *reductio ad absurdum* (literally, “reduction to an absurd thing”). It is also called proof by **refutation** or proof by **contradiction**. One assumes a sentence β to be false and shows that this leads to a contradiction with known axioms α. This contradiction is exactly what is meant by saying that the sentence (α ⋀ ¬β) is unsatisfiable.

* * *

REDUCTIO AD ABSURDUM

* * *

* * *

REFUTATION

* * *

* * *

CONTRADICTION

* * *

![image](img/00006.jpg)

#### **7.5.1 Inference and proofs**

This section covers **inference rules** that can be applied to derive a **proof**—a chain of conclusions that leads to the desired goal. The best-known rule is called **Modus Ponens** (Latin for *mode that affirms*) and is written

> ![equ](img/00183.jpg)



The notation means that, whenever any sentences of the form α ⇒ β and α are given, then the sentence β can be inferred. For example, if `*WumpusAhead*` ⋀`*WumpusAlive*` ⇒ `*Shoot*` and (`*WumpusAhead*` ⋀ `*WumpusAlive*`) are given, then `*Shoot*` can be inferred.

`* * *`

`INFERENCE RULES`

`* * *`

`* * *`

`PROOF`

`* * *`

`* * *`

`MODUS PONENS`

`* * *`

Another useful inference rule is `**And-Elimination**`, which says that, from a conjunction, any of the conjuncts can be inferred:

> ![equ](img/00184.jpg)

For example, from (`*WumpusAhead*` ⋀ `*WumpusAlive*`), `*WumpusAlive*` can be inferred.

`* * *`

`AND-ELIMINATION`

`* * *`

By considering the possible truth values of α and β, one can show easily that Modus Ponens and And-Elimination are sound once and for all. These rules can then be used in any particular instances where they apply, generating sound inferences without the need for enumerating models.

All of the logical equivalences in Figure 7.11 can be used as inference rules. For example, the equivalence for biconditional elimination yields the two inference rules

> ![equ](img/00185.jpg)

Not all inference rules work in both directions like this. For example, we cannot run Modus Ponens in the opposite direction to obtain α ⇒ β and α from β.

Let us see how these inference rules and equivalences can be used in the wumpus world. We start with the knowledge base containing `*R*[1]` through `*R*[5]` and show how to prove ¬`*P*[1,2]`, that is, there is no pit in \[1,2\]. First, we apply biconditional elimination to `*R*[2]` to obtain

> `*R*[6]` : (`*B*[1,1]` ⇒ (`*P*[1,2]` ⋄ `*P*[2,1]`)) ⋀ ((`*P*[1,2]` ⋄ `*P*[2,1]`) ⇒ `*B*[1,1]`).

Then we apply And-Elimination to `*R*[6]` to obtain

> `*R*[7]` : ((`*P*[1,2]` ⋄ `*P*[2,1]`) ⇒ `*B*[1,1]`).

Logical equivalence for contrapositives gives

> `*R*[8]` : (¬`*B*[1,1]` ⇒ ¬(`*P*[1,2]` ⋄ `*P*[2,1]`)).

Now we can apply Modus Ponens with `*R*[8]` and the percept `*R*[4]` (i.e., ¬`*B*[1,1]`), to obtain

> `*R*[9]` : ¬(`*P*[1,2]` ⋄ `*P*[2,1]`).

Finally, we apply De Morgan’s rule, giving the conclusion

> `*R*[10]` : ¬`*P*[1,2]` ⋀ ¬`*P*[2,1]`.

That is, neither \[1,2\] nor \[2,1\] contains a pit.

We found this proof by hand, but we can apply any of the search algorithms in Chapter 3 to find a sequence of steps that constitutes a proof. We just need to define a proof problem as follows:

> • INITIAL STATE: the initial knowledge base.

> • ACTIONS: the set of actions consists of all the inference rules applied to all the sentences that match the top half of the inference rule.

> • RESULT: the result of an action is to add the sentence in the bottom half of the inference rule.

> • GOAL: the goal is a state that contains the sentence we are trying to prove.

Thus, searching for proofs is an alternative to enumerating models. In many practical cases *finding a proof can be more efficient because the proof can ignore irrelevant propositions, no matter how many of them there are.* For example, the proof given earlier leading to ¬`*P*[1,2]` ⋀ ¬`*P*[2,1]` does not mention the propositions `*B*[2,1]`, `*P*[1,1]`, `*P*[2,2]`, or `*P*[3,1]`. They can be ignored because the goal proposition, `*P*[1,2]`, appears only in sentence `*R*[2]`; the other propositions in `*R*[2]` appear only in `*R*[4]` and `*R*[2]`; so `*R*[1]`, `*R*[3]`, and `*R*[5]` have no bearing on the proof. The same would hold even if we added a million more sentences to the knowledge base; the simple truth-table algorithm, on the other hand, would be overwhelmed by the exponential explosion of models.

![image](img/00006.jpg)

One final property of logical systems is **monotonicity**, which says that the set of entailed sentences can only *increase* as information is added to the knowledge base.⁸ For any sentences α and β,

> if `*KB*` ⊨ α then `*KB*` ⋀ β ⊨ α.

* * *

`MONOTONICITY`

* * *

For example, suppose the knowledge base contains the additional assertion β stating that there are exactly eight pits in the world. This knowledge might help the agent draw *additional* conclusions, but it cannot invalidate any conclusion α already inferred—such as the conclusion that there is no pit in \[1,2\]. Monotonicity means that inference rules can be applied whenever suitable premises are found in the knowledge base—the conclusion of the rule must follow *regardless of what else is in the knowledge base*.

#### **7.5.2 Proof by resolution**

We have argued that the inference rules covered so far are *sound*, but we have not discussed the question of *completeness* for the inference algorithms that use them. Search algorithms such as iterative deepening search (page 89) are complete in the sense that they will find any reachable goal, but if the available inference rules are inadequate, then the goal is not reachable—no proof exists that uses only those inference rules. For example, if we removed the biconditional elimination rule, the proof in the preceding section would not go through. The current section introduces a single inference rule, **resolution**, that yields a complete inference algorithm when coupled with any complete search algorithm.

We begin by using a simple version of the resolution rule in the wumpus world. Let us consider the steps leading up to Figure 7.4(a): the agent returns from \[2,1\] to \[1,1\] and then goes to \[1,2\], where it perceives a stench, but no breeze. We add the following facts to the knowledge base:

> `*R*[11]` : ¬`*B*[1,2]`.

> `*R*[12]` : `*B*[1,2]` ⇔ (`*P*[1,1]` ⋄ `*P*[2,2]` ⋄ `*P*[1,3]`).

By the same process that led to `*R*[10]` earlier, we can now derive the absence of pits in \[2,2\] and \[1,3\] (remember that \[1,1\] is already known to be pitless):

> `*R*[13]` : ¬`*P*[2,2].

> `*R*[14]` : ¬`*P*[1,3].

We can also apply biconditional elimination to `*R*[3]`, followed by Modus Ponens with `*R*[5]`, to obtain the fact that there is a pit in \[1,1\], \[2,2\], or \[3,1\]:

> `*R*[15]` : `*P*[1,1]` ⋄ `*P*[2,2]` ⋄ `*P*[3,1]`.

Now comes the first application of the resolution rule: the literal ¬`*P*[2,2]` in `*R*[13]` *resolves with* the literal `*P*[2,2]` in `*R*[15]` to give the **resolvent**

> `*R*[16]` : `*P*[1,1]` ⋄ `*P*[3,1]`.

* * *

`RESOLVENT`

* * *

In English; if there’s a pit in one of \[1,1\], \[2,2\], and \[3,1\] and it’s not in \[2,2\], then it’s in \[1,1\] or \[3,1\]. Similarly, the literal ¬`*P*[1,1]` in `*R*[1]` resolves with the literal `*P*[1,1]` in `*R*[16]` to give

> `*R*[17]` : `*P*[3,1]`.

In English: if there’s a pit in \[1,1\] or \[3,1\] and it’s not in \[1,1\], then it’s in \[3,1\]. These last two inference steps are examples of the **unit resolution** inference rule,

> ![equ](img/00186.jpg)

* * *

`UNIT RESOLUTION`

* * *

where each ℓ is a literal and ℓ[*i*] and *m* are **complementary literals** (i.e., one is the negation of the other). Thus, the unit resolution rule takes a **clause**—a disjunction of literals—and a literal and produces a new clause. Note that a single literal can be viewed as a disjunction of one literal, also known as a **unit clause**.

* * *

`COMPLEMENTARY LITERALS`

* * *

* * *

`CLAUSE`

* * *

* * *

`UNIT CLAUSE`

* * *

The unit resolution rule can be generalized to the full **resolution** rule,

> ![equ](img/00187.jpg)

where ℓ[*i*] and *m*[*j*] are complementary literals. This says that resolution takes two clauses and produces a new clause containing all the literals of the two original clauses *except* the two complementary literals. For example, we have

> ![equ](img/00188.jpg)

* * *

`RESOLUTION`

* * *

There is one more technical aspect of the resolution rule: the resulting clause should contain only one copy of each literal.⁹ The removal of multiple copies of literals is called **factoring**. For example, if we resolve (*A* ⋄ *B*) with (*A* ⋄¬*B*), we obtain (*A* ⋄ *A*), which is reduced to just *A*.

* * *

`FACTORING`

* * *




-   [分辨率规则的可靠性](https://example.org)可以通过考虑互补于句子 *m*[*j*] 的文字 ℓ[*i*] 来轻易观察。如果 ℓ[*i*] 为真，则 *m*[*j*] 为假，因此 *m*[1] ⋁ … ⋁ *m*[*j*−1] ⋁ *m*[*j*+1] ⋁ … ⋁ *m*[*n*] 必须为真，因为 *m*[1] ⋁ … ⋁ *m*[*n*] 已经给出。如果 ℓ[*i*] 为假，则 ℓ[1] ⋁ … ⋁ ℓ[*i*−1] ⋁ [*i*+1] ⋁ … ⋁ ℓ[*k*] 必须为真，因为 ℓ[1] ⋁ … ⋁ ℓ[*k*] 已经给出。现在 ℓ[*i*] 要么为真，要么为假，所以其中一个结论成立——正好符合分辨率规则的要求。

![image](img/00006.jpg)

分辨率规则更令人惊讶的是，它是一系列 `complete` 推理程序的基础。`A` 分辨率基于命题逻辑的句子 α 和 β，可以决定 α ⊨ β。下面的两个子节解释了分辨率如何实现这一点。

##### **合取范式**

分辨率规则仅适用于句子（即字面量的析取），因此似乎只适用于由句子组成的知识库和查询。那么，它如何能导致命题逻辑中所有句子的完整推理程序呢？答案是：每一个命题逻辑句子都与一个句子的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的析取的
